## Page 1


SCALAR ANOMALOUS DISSIPATION AND OPTIMAL REGULARITY
VIA ITERATED HOMOGENIZATION
JAN BURCZAK, L´ASZL´O SZ´EKELYHIDI, JR., AND BIAN WU
Abstract. For any β0 < 1/3 we construct divergence free vector fields in Cβ0
x,t and a se-
quence of diffusivities κq ↘0 such that, for an arbitrary initial datum from a low regularity
class, the classical solution ρq to the advection-diffusion equation exhibits anomalous dissi-
pation along the sequence κq. At the same time ρq remains uniformly bounded in C0
t Cα0
x ,
where β0 + 2α0 < 1. Our result confirms a conjecture of Armstrong and Vicol [4] and shows
sharpness of the Obukhov-Corrsin threshold within the context of iterated homogenization.
Our construction confirms time-homogeneity of the dissipation anomaly, as required in tur-
bulence theory, and as a consequence we also obtain better time regularity for the scalar ρq
than the classical prediction of Yaglom.

Theorem 1.1.1 is a substantial extension of the main result proved in [4] via iterated
homogenization: the first statement above (1.1.2) is the subject of [4, Theorem 1.1], but
the second statement above (1.1.3) has merely been conjectured to hold in [4, Section 1.3],
and is in fact the main novelty of our work. We also remark that the first statement above
in the 3D case has been substantially improved in our prior work [10], where (i) u is in
addition a weak solution of the 3D Euler equations satisfying an h-principle, and (ii) using
the explicit dependence of c0 on the data and based on the fluctuation-dissipation relation
introduced in [19], estimate (1.1.2) is complemented with a lower bound on the average rate
of separation between two realizations of the associated stochastic differential equation of
(1.0.1), in agreement with the Richardson law of dispersion.
1.2. Context. In hydrodynamic turbulence theory, the advection-diffusion equation (1.0.1)
serves as an important toy model for ’turbulent transport’. For example, the classical K41
theory of turbulence based on the 3D Navier-Stokes equations finds its analogy in the Corrsin-
Obukhov-Yaglom theory for the advection-diffusion equation. There are two foundational
postulates common to both of these theories [25, Section 6]1:
(I) finite non-vanishing mean rate of dissipation in the inviscid limit;
(II) homogeneity and isotropy on small scales.
In the context of advection-diffusion (1.0.1) postulate (I) translates, in its most basic form,
into the statement that

’anomalous dissipation’. Examples of divergence-free vector fields u for which one can verify
(1.2.1) have been constructed in [18] and shortly after in [11, 20] with optimal regularity
properties (see Section 1.2.1 below for a more in-depth discussion). The drawback of these

1.2.1. Optimal regularity. The central prediction of K41 theory for Navier-Stokes turbulence,
based on the hypothesis of anomalous dissipation, is a precise scaling law for the energy spec-
trum in the inertial range: E(k) ∼c2/3
0
k−5/3. A mathematical formulation of this prediction
has famously been put forward by L. Onsager in 1949, which became known as Onsager’s
conjecture and is concerned with ’ideal turbulence’: the threshold regularity for conserva-
tion of energy in the Euler equations is precisely C1/3 on the H¨older scale (corresponding
to monofractal scaling). We refer to [22] for an excellent recent survey on Onsager’s ideal
turbulence and the current state of the art. The corresponding prediction in the context of
passive scalar advection-diffusion is the Obukhov-Corrsin law and can be stated in the most
basic form as follows [38, 14], see also [18]: if the advecting velocity field u ∈Cβ is H¨older
continuous with exponent α > 0, then the solution ρκ of (1.0.1) should be ρκ ∈Cα uniformly
in κ > 0, with β + 2α = 1, and not better. A more precise form of this prediction was
obtained by Yaglom [42], see also [11], stating that the criticality threshold for anomalous
dissipation is

This result of [11] has been extended by Elgindi and Liss in [20] (based on ideas in [18]) at the

Compared to these works, the novelty of our contribution is twofold: firstly, our estimates
(1.1.3), (1.1.1) not only saturate the Obukhov-Corrsin relation from below, but in fact yield
better time regularity. More importantly, we are able to achieve this in combination with
the stronger, time-homogeneous form of anomalous dissipation (1.1.2). In this respect the
work of Armstrong and Vicol was a major step forward in the field as it not only achieved
(1.1.2) in agreement with postulate (II), but also identified iterated homogenization as the
correct mechanism behind the phenomenon of anomalous dissipation (in agreement with the

classical idea of eddy diffusivity). However, the authors of [4] left open the question of uniform
regularity of the density, and conjectured that with their approach, combined with “large-

regularity of ρq uniformly in q (in agreement with the Obukhov-Corrsin prediction).
In this paper we confirm this conjecture. Our methods complement the ideas introduced
in [4], but we do not follow the ’large scale regularity’ approach suggested there. Instead, we
develop a novel framework for iterated quantitative homogenization for high-order correctors.
1.2.2. Outlook. We believe that this paper does not only contain a new result in the fine
analysis of the advection-diffusion equation and in anomalous dissipation of scalar variance,
but provides a set of useful and sharp technical tools. In particular, we believe these tools
can allow to gain insights into the following themes, which we plan to address in a subsequent
work:
(P.1) Spatial homogeneity of anomalous dissipation. We expect the anomalous dissipation
constructed in Theorem 1.1 to be homogeneous both in space and in time, which would
amount to complete justification of postulate (II). In technical terms, we expect that for any
nonempty open parabolic cylinder E ⊂T2 × [0, 1] and generic initial datum,
lim inf
q→∞κq
¨
E
|∇ρq|2dxdt ≥c(E, ρin, u).
(1.2.6)
A further interesting question is then to characterize the dependence of c(E, ρin, u) on E, ρin
and u. We expect that the techniques in this paper will help such characterization.
(P.2) H-principle and Euler equations. Based on the work [10] bridging iterated homog-
enization in [4] and convex integration in [9], we expect the existence of a large class of
turbulent vector fields u, exhibiting both homogeneous anomalous dissipation (in the sense
of (P.1)) and optimal H¨older regularity for scalar fields, which are dense in an appropriate
sense. This would yield an h-principle for these vector fields. Moreover, we expect they can
be constructed as H¨older continuous weak solutions to the incompressible Euler equations,
given the insights from convex integration [16, 33, 9, 26].
1.2.3. Related works. In the recent past there has been a surge of activity concerning the
mathematical foundation of the phenomenon of anomalous dissipation and the closely re-
lated issue of mixing. Although a rigorous approach for the nonlinear Navier-Stokes case is
still widely open, the case of the advection-diffusion equation (1.0.1) has seen several very
interesting advances. Beyond the works already mentioned we wish to point out [15, 21, 43,
31, 32, 35, 39, 41, 45]. Finally, we mention the excellent surveys [17, 22] for a wider overview
and context of the subject.
1.3. Challenges and main ideas. Our construction of the vector field u in Theorem 1.1
essentially follows the approach of [4, 10], arising iteratively from a sequence {uq}, where
uq+1 = uq + wq+1 and wq+1 is a highly oscillatory perturbation with spatial frequency λq+1
and temporal frequency µq+1. The perturbation wq+1 is transported by uq locally in time
intervals of length µ−1
q+1 - see for instance Section 2.3. In a first approximation, we can think
of such a perturbation as taking the form
wq+1(x, t) = aq(x, t)η(µq+1t)W

In this way we obtain a sequence of advection-diffusion equations, with advecting vector
field uq and diffusion coefficient κq ∼λ−θ

with θ ∼1 + β, such that at each level q the
deformation due to transport and the diffusion are balanced. In oversimplified terms the
corresponding solutions ρq arise then from iteratively homogenizing equation q+1 to equation

OPTIMAL REGULARITY IN SCALAR ANOMALOUS DISSIPATION
5
q, see [10, Section 2]. Looking at the explicit form of the first order corrector and basic energy
estimates in [4, 10] suggests then the following naive approach to regularity: obtain inductive
information on higher regularity of ρq+1 −ρq in the form of Campanato bounds, and then to
apply the tools developed for the ”large scale regularity” theory in stochastic homogenization
[2]. However, this strategy seems to run into two fundamental difficulties:
• on small scales ∼λ−1
q
we have to deal with a lack of sufficiently large scale separa-
tion: The scale of separation is at most λq/λq+1, which is very mild compared to the
degeneracy in ellipticity κq+1. In order to achieve the optimal H¨older regularity, we
thus need to compute correctors in the homogenization procedure to a certain very
high order Q.
• in contrast, on larger scales we see that the background transport uq remains at
order 1, while the ellipticity coefficient κq degenerates to zero.
As well shall see
later, this ”high contrast environment” means that energy estimates and Gr¨onwall
or Schauder estimates are far from being sufficient for pursuing any useful uniform
H¨older regularity of ρq.
Although the first issue is possibly more technical than fundamental, since high order ex-
pansions are by now well known in periodic homogenization and also in quasi-periodic or
stochastic settings [29], the second issue seems to be a fundamental feature of our setup, pro-
hibiting the use of ”large-scale regularity” techniques [5, 6]. Instead, we are forced to develop
a version of iterated homogenization which includes high order expansions. In turn this leads
to new challenges, which are of a different nature from those encountered in quantitative
stochastic homogenization [27, 24, 2, 1, 3].
1.4. Main ingredients of our approach. To prove Theorem 1.1, we establish a regularity
theory for these turbulent vector fields via higher order correctors. Below, we summarize the
main new ingredients in this work.
1.4.1. Expanding the flow maps to reveal higher order periodicity in time. Even though, as
explained above, our velocity fluctuations wq+1 are heuristically expected to be periodic in
space and time as in (1.3.1), this is only true upto first order. In fact, the nature of temporal
cutoffs and the use of local-in-time flow maps means that the actual fluctuations are not
periodic, neither in space nor in time. For the purposes of [4, 10] this is sufficient, as effects
from lower order terms have a negligible contribution in the energy norm L∞
t L2
x. However,
for the higher order corrector expansion these terms do become significant and they may lead
to non-negligible contributions.

map Φq arising from a temporal Taylor expansion of the local flow maps

The expansion above reduces our problem, at each iteration step, to a cleaner homogenization
problem up to arbitrarily small perturbations, at least formally, which is very desirable.
1.4.2. Single space-time hybrid homogenization problem. With above expansion on flow maps,
at each iteration step, we can focus on the following over-simplified advection diffusion equa-
tion in T2 × [0, 1],

From classical homogenization theory [8, 44, 40], we know there are three types of correctors
that we have to consider. The first type is the spatial corrector, which captures the effect of
fast spatial oscillation λ−1
q+1. The second type is the temporal corrector, which captures the
effect of temporal oscillation µ−1
q+1. The third type is the boundary corrector, which is needed
to enforce initial condition on parabolic boundary {t = 0}.
Obtaining a high order expansion for such equations seems highly delicate, because now we
have 2 distinct small scales λ−1
q+1 and µ−1
q+1 in addition to the large scale, with no apriori well-
defined ordering among different powers. In other words, there is not a unqiue way to even
compute a formal asymptotic expansion. We present an algorithmic way to simultaneously
compute all three types of correctors up to arbitrarily high order. The details are presented
in Section 6.5 and Section 6.6.
1.4.3. Chain of homogenization problems, renormalized differential operators and polynomial-
derivative space. As mentioned above, at each step q we require an expansion of ρq+1 up to
very high order Q, forcing us to solve Q different homogenization problems at step q. But
then this means we need to solve Q2 homogenization problems at step q −1. When q goes
to infinity, this would require us to solve infinitely many homogenization problems at step 1,
which is not feasible.
To overcome this issue, we solve a chain of Q2 problems at step q + 1. We solve the first Q
problems by expansion techniques in Section 1.4.2 and the remaining Q2 −Q problems by the
energy method. This is possible because of a special structure of the chains, consisting of two
parts: the divergence form of the Q homogenized problems and a special algebraic structure of
function coefficients, introduced by the deformation of the flow maps ∇Φq. In particular, the
function coefficients in the homogenized problems are polynomials of ∇Φq and its derivatives.
To efficiently implement estimates and capture their algebraic structure, we use renormalized
differential operators and introduce a special polynomial-derivative space. The purpose is to
be able to efficiently propagate both their algebraic structure and the estimates on chains of
homogenization problems when passing from step q + 1 to step q. These tools are developed
in Section 2.4, Section 3 and Section 4. The homogenization schemes are then presented in
Section 6 and 7.
1.4.4. Inertial range, dissipative range and scale-by-scale decomposition of initial datum. In
Section 1.4.1, Section 1.4.2 and Section 1.4.3, we have described the main new ingredients in
a single homogenization step. Next, we briefly describe how to implement these iteratively
in q and how to deal with arbitrary initial datum in H1+α′ containing small scales. There
are two key differences with respect to [4, 10].
First, instead of viewing the initial datum containing large scales as a single entity, we
decompose the initial datum into infinitely many energy shells a’la Littlewood-Paley adapted


## Page 7


OPTIMAL REGULARITY IN SCALAR ANOMALOUS DISSIPATION
7
to the scales {λq}q. For a single shell component, we inductively apply the homogenization
expansion to resolve its energy spectrum.
Secondly, we do homogenization separately in the inertial range and the dissipative range.
The inertial range is where the diffusivity is significantly enhanced, amounting to a large eddy
effect. The dissipative range contains scales below the Kolmogorov scale. This approach is
required largely due to the challenges mentioned in Section 1.3.

We also define a support condition.
Definition 2.4 (Support condition). A function η : T →R has support disjoint to η1, η2 if
supp η ∩supp η1 = supp η ∩supp η2 = ∅.
(2.3.19)
Definition 2.5 (Shear condition). A pair of functions (χ, η) with χ : T2 →R, η : T →R
satisfy the shear condition if either
supp η ⊂supp η1,
∂ξ2χ = 0,
or
supp η ⊂supp η2,
∂ξ1χ = 0.
(2.3.20)
A pair of functions (χ, η) satisfy the generalized shear condition if either it satisfies shear
condition or
∇ξχ = 0.
(2.3.21)

in final formulas, we choose the upper bound for all of them, i.e. N, and extend shorter sums
with dummy entries.

25
Dα

(iii. Propagation of derivatives). To sum up, we have to deal with loss of derivative in case
of pure space derivatives, and not in the case of mixed derivatives.
There are two standard remedies to the derivative loss. Either one mollifies the quantities
involved, which allows appropriately high derivative estimates to be given not by (lossy) in-
ductive assumptions, but also by mollified quantities; or one chooses to propagate all deriva-
tives.
In the former case, one needs to quantify the difference between the original and
mollified quantities, in the latter one needs to quantify and control the arising combinatorial
coefficients (appearing for instance in product rule, see e.g. (3.2.4) with its ∼n, or in the chain
rule). We will follow the latter approach, which has been used in [4].
Therefore we propagate all space derivatives, but we don’t have to propagate all mixed
derivatives, which allows us also to be less careful about constants in the mixed derivative
case.

3.3. Proof of Lemma 3.3 and its Corollaries.
3.3.1. Initial remarks. Proof of Lemma 3.3 uses in many places reasonings very similar to
proof of Lemma 3.2, which we invoke without repeating fully. In particular, we will use
Remark 3.17 (Remark on commutativity). As observed in the proof of Lemma 3.2, even
though the space vs advective derivatives did not commute, since the estimates for each type
of derivative were consistent regardless of the order of derivation, the resulting inequalities
were as if the derivatives commuted.
There is, however, one important difference between the currently wanted estimates for
Ψq+1 and the already proven in Lemma 3.2 estimates for ¯Ψq+1. Namely, the estimates on
¯Ψq+1 follow from an inductive procedure, so in essence from estimates on ¯Ψq. Therefore
we were careful concerning derivative loss, since otherwise induction over q ∈N fails (looses
eventually all derivatives). However, the estimates for Ψq+1 involve essentially the estimate
for ¯Ψq+1 and no induction is used. (So ’loss of derivative’ Y →Y −N∗−1 is not related to
inductive step Ψq →Ψq+1, but to a ’same step comparison’ between Ψq+1 and ¯Ψq+1).

4. Decomposition lemmas
As we see in Section 2.4, the vector fields at step q generate oscillatory functions docu-
mented by polynomial-derivative space Pq and Oq at step q. In this section, we quantitatively
decompose the elements in Oq+1 into products of elements in Pq and known oscillatory pro-
files.

5.6. Proof of Proposition 5.4.
5.6.1. Notational remarks. We will use at times slightly informal language, to avoid too
cumbersome notation. In particular
(i. Concerning derivatives) Instead of an arbitrary partial space derivative of order k, we
simply write ∇k; consequently ’·’ between high-order derivatives needs to be interpreted
appropriately.
(ii. Concerning combinatorial coefficients) We will write a ∼β
P
i bi if a = P
i cβ(i)bi, where
cβ(i) are coefficients uniformly bounded in terms of |β| (or a ≲|β|
P
i bi in case of estimates).
Since the coefficient cβ(i) will originate from combinatorial coefficients of a product rule, we
will refer to cβ(i)’s as to ’combinatorial-type coefficients’.
5.6.2. Case of pure space derivatives. The estimate (5.3.1) for pure space derivatives can be
readily obtained along the lines of [10]. Indeed, inspecting the proof of Lemma 4.1 [10] (see
the last inequality in the proof) and taking into account Corollary 4.2 [10] (which includes
forcing), together with Young inequality to estimate the forcing term, we have

derivatives needed: in the worst case, when estimating U-many advective derivatives, we

all our estimates have a huge constant in ≲, which is however merely U-dependent.

6. Homogenization in inertial range
In this section, we prove the homogenization estimates from step q to step q + 1.
6.1. Characterization of initial datum. We introduce a few notions to characterize initial
datum. We need two types of notions, to measure the initial datum at L2 level, and to control
the derivatives of initial datum up to order 14Q3.
At L2-level, we define energy dissipation as follows.

and κ > 0, we use | · |D(u,κ) to denote energy dissipation for initial datum ρin : T2 →R

Remark 6.30. For spatial and temporal correctors, we may have none, one, or multiple correc-
tors for fixed n, a and differentiation index α. For spatial correctors in (6.5.3), the cardinality
of such correctors is denoted by ˆI(α)
n−a ∈N. When ˆI(α)
n−a = 0, this means we do not have cor-
rectors for such n, a and α. The same also applies to temporal correctors.
Remark 6.31 (Stationarity of the correctors). Note that the structural information of these
correctors, such as ˆI(α)
n−a, ˆh(α,l)
n−a , ˆη(α,l)
n−a and ˆχ(α,l)
n−a depend on the integer n −a, instead of n or
a. For example, this means the structural information in first order corrector ˆρ1 is shared
in higher order correctors ˆρn with n ≥2. We refer this property as the stationarity of the
correctors. We state the expansion lemma in a way that stationarity is claimed implicitly.
However, this property does not come for free, and we shall prove it later.

in Lemma 6.29.

6.6. Proof of the expansion lemmas. In this section, we prove the expansion Lemma
6.29 and Lemma 6.35, also Remark 6.31 and Remark 6.36, in three steps.
The first step is given in Section 6.6.1, we show that it suffices to solve two asymptotic
systems of differential equations, respectively for Lemma 6.29 and Lemma 6.35.
The asymptotic systems, obtained in Section 6.6.1 are solved by induction. The second
step is to solve the initial steps of these asymptotic systems for Lemma 6.29 and Lemma 6.35.
This is done separately in Section 6.6.2 and Section 6.6.3.
The third step is the induction for solving the asymptotic systems for both expansion lem-
mas. Specifically, we solve for residual correctors, temporal correctors and spatial correctors,
respectively in Section 6.6.4, Section 6.6.5 and Section 6.6.6. The proofs of Lemma 6.29 and
Lemma 6.35 are completely analogous, so we only present it for Lemma 6.29 and comment
on their minor difference.
6.6.1. Set up the asymptotic system for Lemma 6.29 and Lemma 6.35. We write (6.3.8) in
the following form

6.7. Technical preparation for remainders. One task we shall do later is to estimate the
remainders in expansion lemmas in Section 6.5. Specifically, we need to estimate ˜ρ in Lemma
6.29 and Lemma 6.35. In this section, we prove some technical lemmas to prepare for this
task.

7. Homogenization in dissipative range
In this section, we prove the inductive homogenization estimates from step q to step q+1 for
dissipative range. In dissipative range, the difference between eddy diffusivity and molecular
diffusivity is very small and can be treated as a perturbation. Throughout this section, we
work with diffusivity κ ≥0 satisfying
κ ≥κq.

The proof strategy of Theorem 7.2 is the same as Theorem 6.10. Since we have a relatively
large diffusivity κ, the expansion lemma and its proof can be simplified. At heuristic level,
since the difference between eddy diffusivity and molecular diffusivity is very small, we treat
this difference as a perturbation. Hence, we do not need to solve cell problem in time in
homogenization.

Sketch of proof to Theorem 7.2. Compared with Theorem 6.10, the difference in Theorem 7.2
is only due to the larger diffusivity κ.
To prove Theorem 7.2, it suffices to verify two main ingredients. The first ingredient is
unconditional energy estimates, in the form of Proposition 6.23 and Proposition 6.28. From
Lemma 5.10, Lemma 5.13 and Lemma 5.14, these obvious hold, since the larger diffusivity
yields stronger energy estimates.
The second ingredient is the homogenization estimates via asymptotic expansion. The
whole homogenization analysis is essentially consequences of Lemma 6.29 and Lemma 6.35.
And Lemma 6.35 is a variant of Lemma 6.29. Therefore, it boils down to prove Lemma 7.4,
which is the variant of Lemma 6.29 in the dissipative range.
□

7.4. Proof of Lemma 7.4. In this section, we prove Lemma 7.4. The proof is largely similar
to the proof of Lemma 6.29, except for two different points. The first point is that we choose
to deal with easier cell problems, which are set up in Section 7.4.1 below. The second point
is the different parameter relations, hence we need to verify the expansion converges under
these parameter relations. This is done in Section 7.4.2, Section 7.4.3 and Section 7.4.4. We
shall omit many other similar details.

u ∈Cβ0(T2 × [0, 1]) such that (1.1.2) and (1.1.3) with α = α0 holds.
